#2771. Planes, trains and automobiles: The best ways to beat travel fatigue—so you can come home feeling rested and fulfilled

Five-hour flights. Eleven-hour road trips. Fifteen-hour train rides. All potentially exhausting options for the annual tradition of convening around the dinner table with loved ones on Thanksgiving.

An estimated 81.5 million Americans are projected to travel, or have already traveled, at least 50 miles from their homes between Nov. 24 and Nov. 30, according to the American Automobile Association. And their mode of transportation is shifting: Fewer people are flying or driving this year compared to 2025, and more are traveling by buses, trains and cruises, AAA data shows.

High gas prices and soaring plane ticket costs may factor into the shift. As of Wednesday evening, fuel prices are roughly 17% higher than they were on the same date in 2025, according to AAA data. Meanwhile, "months of elevated jet fuel costs due to the conflict in Iran have contributed to Thanksgiving and Christmas airfares reaching 10-year highs," according to fare-tracking platform Hopper.

The alternate modes of transit may open travelers up to new experiences — and new forms of travel fatigue to keep you from returning home feeling rested and fulfilled. You're probably already familiar with the exhaustion you might get from a day of plane travel, for example. In airplanes, "there can be lower humidity, maybe lower oxygen levels at cabin pressure. Sometimes people report feeling more dehydrated or drained. You're adjusting to that change in altitude," says Alaina Tiani, a Cleveland Clinic behavioral sleep medicine psychologist.

But even waiting in places like train stations or cruise ports can feel draining due to sheer sensory overload, Tiani says. And then, depending on your mode of travel, you may still need to deal with cramped seating and limited ability to get up and walk around or drink some water and eat a snack.

Once you arrive at your destination, managing responsibilities like remembering where you put your hotel key and deciding what to eat and how to structure your day can also take a mental toll, says travel writer Shannon O'Donnell, who's written about managing travel fatigue. "All of that actually requires a lot more thinking and planning for your brain," she says, adding: "People just don't understand how those add up and why a relaxing vacation suddenly has them feeling wrecked."

The consequences can sneak up on you, O'Donnell notes. Travel fatigue can "present as maybe a lack of interest in something that you might usually find fascinating," she says. Instead of looking out the window on a plane ride or playing car games with your kids on a road trip, you might disengage and appear apathetic to the people around you.

You might stand a better chance of a fulfilling, restful holiday if you prepare for your travel fatigue. Here, Tiani and O'Donnell share their advice for how to do it:

#2769. Top Wall Street analysts favor these 3 dividend stocks for strong returns

Investors often favor dividend stocks for their regular income and the promise of potential share price appreciation. But selecting the right dividend stocks from a huge pool of companies can be difficult.

Top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend-paying stocks, as their recommendations are backed by thorough analysis of a company's financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Devon Energy (DVN), an independent oil and natural gas exploration and production company, reported better-than-expected third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of 32 cents per share for the fourth quarter, payable December 30. Based on the fixed dividends paid over the trailing 12 months, DVN yields 2.85%.

After hosting a virtual investor meeting with management, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Devon, with a price target of $59. Mehta said that he is now more confident in management's outlook for delivering strong near-term capital returns as well as efforts to drive operational efficiencies and productivity gains.

Devon sees an opportunity to increase share repurchases, driven by solid free cash flow (FCF), contributions from the Eagle Ford divestiture and continued improvement in the balance sheet, Mehta said. Devon expects share buybacks worth more than $1 billion in Q4 2026, up from $414 million in the third quarter, backed by a strong pricing backdrop and improved capital efficiency.

Mehta, a 5-star analyst, said Devon expects about $6.3 billion of excess FCF in 2027, including about $3.5 billion of net proceeds from the Eagle Ford sale. That would enable the company to accelerate buybacks while progressing toward its goal of reducing gross debt to about $9 billion by early 2027. Mehta noted that under DVN's framework to return up to 70% of annual FCF, management favors share buybacks as it believes the stock is discounted compared to large-cap peers.

"Management expects continued capital efficiency gains from merger synergies, extended laterals, surfactant adoption and development of the newly acquired federal acreage," Mehta said.

Mehta ranks No. 876 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 8.1%. See Devon Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Energy Transfer (ET), is a limited partnership that operates about 140,000 miles of pipeline and associated energy infrastructure. The company recently announced third-quarter results showing net income per common unit growing about 18% year-over-year, to 33 cents. ET's cash distribution for Q3 2026 was 34.25 cents per common unit, up more than 3% year-over-year. At an annualized distribution of $1.37 per share, Energy Transfer yields more than 7%.

TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Energy Transfer with a price target of $25. The company delivered better-than-expected Q3 results and announced the 1.2 billion cubic feet per day (bcf/d) South Mississippi Pipeline, a 215-mile, 36-inch natural gas line linking the Perryville Hub to the Florida Gas Transmission (FGT) network, Gabelman said. The project is supported by 20-year firm transportation agreements and is expected to enter service in 2030. ET expects to ultimately own 51% of the $1.6 billion project.

The 5-star analyst said that Energy Transfer lowered its 2026 capital spending outlook to $5.5 billion (midpoint of the guidance range) from $5.75 billion due to project timing. Energy Transfer expects capex to rise to $7 billion in 2027, while Gabelman projects $5 billion in both 2028 and 2029, based on disclosed projects.

Gabelman also highlighted management's commentary about optimization initiatives contributing an incremental $800 million to 2026 earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization, a larger benefit than in recent years. "A return to less commodity volatility should the [Middle East] war end could bring some relative downside, though this is offset by continued strong growth prospects," he said.

Gabelman ranks No. 397 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.4%. See Energy Transfer KPIs on TipRanks.

Oil and gas exploration and production company ConocoPhillips (COP) delivered better-than-expected Q3 performance. The company announced an 11% hike in its quarterly dividend to 93 cents per share, effective with the Q4 dividend payable on December 1. Based on the dividends paid over the trailing 12 months, COP yields about 2.6%.

Following the Q3 results, Wells Fargo analyst Sam Margolin repeated a buy rating on ConocoPhillips, citing strong potential for further dividend increases. Margolin also trimmed his price target to $185 from $189 to reflect timing effects and the WAHA forward curve, though he said that COP could still re-rate as its natural portfolio lifecycle provides stronger protection against inflationary pressures than peers. The Waha forward curve tracks future natural gas prices based on the West Texas Waha hub.

Margolin, another 5-star analyst, highlighted that ConocoPhillips' Q3 2026 adjusted earnings per share of $3.27 was well above Wells Fargo and the Street's estimates of $3.04 and $3.05, respectively. Earnings were driven by solid price realizations, partially offset by higher depletion, depreciation and amortization and purchased commodities.

Margolin also noted COP's dividend increase and said the accelerated buyback enabled by current commodity prices could further support long-term dividend growth. He sees the possibility of ConocoPhillips' dividend growth rate exceeding its current 6% cost of equity into the end of the decade. Margolin expects COP to sustain an 8% dividend compound annual growth rate through 2029 while repurchasing $6.4 billion of stock annually, and reducing net debt-to-capital to less than 9% by the end of 2028 based on the assumption of an average Brent crude oil price of $75 a barrel.

"The message on the 3Q call was similar to that of 2Q, reiterating the moderation in reinvestment rate and commitment to dividend growth through the Willow start-up and after," Margolin said.

Margolin ranks No. 189 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 69% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.6%. See ConocoPhillips Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2768. Trump, NATO chief caution Russia as attacks on Ukraine’s energy infrastructure intensify

U.S. President Donald Trump and NATO chief Mark Rutte issued a joint statement on Thursday urging Russia not to take any actions, intentionally or otherwise, that would further expand the Ukraine war.

The statement comes as Ukraine's allies sound the alarm over Russia's growing threat to European security and just one week after Trump's historic trilateral meeting with Russian President Vladimir Putin and Chinese President Xi Jinping in Asia.

Trump and Rutte described their meeting at the Mar-a-Lago club in Palm Beach, Florida on Wednesday as "very impactful," with Russia's nearly five-year-long war in Ukraine and the Iran war among the key topics of conversation.

"We also discussed the importance of Russia avoiding any actions that intentionally, or unintentionally, expand the war, and the importance of obtaining a peaceful resolution to the war as rapidly as possible," Trump and Rutte said in their statement.

Moreover, the two discussed the importance of further increasing defense spending as well as investment in the defense industrial base in both the U.S. and NATO countries, according to the statement.

Trump also said in a post on Truth Social Thursday that he "briefed NATO on the tremendous progress" being made in growing defense production, noting that the focus will be on building Patriot air defense systems, THAAD systems and Tomahawk cruise missiles, as well as "many new Weapons, both Offensive and Defensive, that have not yet been viewed by the Public, or other Countries."

"There has never been a time when the United States has produced more Military Equipment or Munitions than now. Our 'cupboard' will soon be full and, when it is, our Allies and Partners throughout the World will be benefitting greatly," he said in the post.

Trump's meeting with Xi and Putin, in which he described the Russian president as his "friend," sparked alarm among European lawmakers about Washington's commitment to transatlantic security.

NATO's Rutte, a seasoned diplomat known as a consensus builder during his nearly 14 years as Dutch prime minister, has repeatedly sought to de-escalate tensions in the alliance and frequently used flattery to win over the U.S. president.

While some have questioned whether this approach has delivered any tangible benefits, Trump has previously described Rutte as a "great leader" and NATO's "biggest asset."

Russia has stepped up its attacks on Ukraine's energy infrastructure in recent weeks, with further attacks on the central Ukrainian city of Kryvyi Rih overnight disrupting power supplies and bringing municipal electric public transport to a standstill, according to Oleksandr Vilkul, head of Kryvyi ​Rih's defense council.

Ukrainian officials on Wednesday said Russian attacks on the country's power sector had damaged energy infrastructure and caused power outages in five regions as cold winter weather sets in.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz on Wednesday accused the Kremlin of "escalating" threats toward European security, citing actions in the Baltic region, Poland and on the Black Sea, and adding that "Russia knows exactly what it wants. And Russia wants more than Ukraine."

A spokesperson for Russia's Foreign Ministry did not immediately respond to a CNBC request for comment on Thursday morning. The Kremlin has repeatedly denied seeking military confrontation with NATO countries.

#2767. De’Anthony Melton stuffs the box score in Warriors’ pre-Thanksgiving win

On the first Thanksgiving, the overconfident Plymouth Rock settlers had to lean on the Wampanoag tribe to survive their first harvest in the New World. There's scant historical evidence about that first Thanksgiving, but some scholars believe that the Pilgrim colony found itself at a loss after Miles Standish suffered an abdominal oblique strain, one that wouldn't be evaluated until the Friday following Thanksgiving, a holiday that wouldn't exist for another quarter-millenium.

On the Wednesday before Thanksgiving 2026, the Golden State Warriors got 61 points from their bench players and eight three-pointers from De'Anthony Melton as they beat the Sacramento Kings, 129-110. Thanksgiving may not be a holiday in Brazil, but Gui Santos still feasted Wednesday night, putting up 22 points in 24 minutes.

Draymond Green had 11 assists, Yaxel Lendeborg had 15 points and eight rebounds and Brandin Podziemski notched a +16 in 32 minutes, but the Warriors won their game by dominating the bench minutes. Granted, bringing Leaky Black and Precious Achiuwa to an NBA game is like bringing a knife to a gun fight, especially if the knife turned the ball over 18 times, but the bench dominated, 61-19, to get the Warriors their second straight win.

That's intended in no way to minimize Melton, who is now shooting 44.9% from three-point range this season. He went 8-for-12 from distance Wednesday against the Kings and added seven assists, which just goes to show that any time you get the chance to have surgeries on both thumbs in one summer, you pull the damn trigger.

It was a game when the Warriors played well against type, taking a large 1st-quarter lead instead of falling hopelessly behind after eight minutes, a hallmark of their recent road trip. But they were back at the Chase Center, where the Warriors had won seven straight games after choking the home opener against the Memphis Grizzlies. And as the poet says, there's no place like home for the holidays.

Melton shot 4-for-5 from behind the arc in the opening period as the Warriors raced out to a 25-point lead after 12 minutes. They went 9-for-13 as a team and 14-for-22 overall, with Brandin Podziemski and Gui Santos each shooting 3-for-3 with two triples in the quarter.

Brandon Williams wasted no time in his first game in 16 days, scoring 14 points in 13 minutes, while putting up 14 shots and delivering three assists. That's officially a usage rate of "Thank God," and should help Williams when he lobbies the Chase Center's operations staff to change the current "road runner" sound effect when he scores to a Ferrari engine revving. He's now played 150 NBA games yet he already thinks he's too good for the road runner, a bird that has faced off against Wile E. Coyote and survived over a hundred times. Show him some repect!

In the second quarter, Melton made two more threes and Yaxel Lendeborg scored eight points to keep the halftime lead at 19 points. Melton also allowed Williams to use his laundry facilities this week, likely giving his teammate the confidence to dominate the ball the way he has. Williams does have his own apartment now, and more than perhaps any other Warrior needs them to advance to the prize money stage of the NBA Cup.

On the Sacramento side, Domantas Sabonis scored 27 points while taking only nine shots, also picking up 10 rebounds. Unfortunately for Sabonis, it's not clear if playing well or badly will get him what he truly wants: The chance to play for a team who isn't the 2-16 Kings.

Rookie Darius Acuff, Jr. had a high-volume, low-efficiency game, scoring 22 points on 9-for-24 shooting and logging nine assists. It's been a Make-A-Wish type season for Acuff, where the 20-year-old has been able to live his NBA dream of shooting whenever he wants, only it's also like he wished on a monkey's paw, because he's on a team that has won only two games all season.

But Gui Santos stood out with his first game of 20+ points all season, punishing smaller, slower, or just plain lazier Kings defenders to shoot 8-for-11 and score 22 points.

The Warriors are back up to 10-8, stuffing themselves on victories before heading to Portland Friday, where they'll face a team likely woozy from eating vegan turkey and giving out land acknowledgements all day Thursday. Could it be a three-game win streak for the Warriors? Maybe it's enough that the team is all together on a holiday. And still alive on the NBA Cup, which could even let Williams finally own his own washer.

#2766. Raiders QB Fernando Mendoza thankful for Aaron Rodgers on Thanksgiving

Somewhere in the middle of airing his grievances with the Pittsburgh Steelers on Wednesday, Aaron Rodgers took a moment to say something nice about a fellow quarterback.

Imagine Fernando Mendoza's surprise to find out it was him.

Rodgers was on "The Pat McAfee Show" explaining how things had gone sideways with Steelers coach Mike McCarthy when he got around to the Denver film. Pittsburgh plays the Broncos on Friday. The Raiders had just been through Denver on Sunday, which is how a rookie making his second NFL start earned a cameo in somebody else's very public breakup.

Las Vegas Raiders quarterback Fernando Mendoza (15) throws against the Denver Broncos during an NFL football game in Denver, Sunday, Nov. 22, 2026. AP Photo/Jack Dempsey

"And I'll tell you: Hey, the kid Mendoza, man. He was running around, making some plays in the scramble."

Funny thing about that tape. If Rodgers goes back to the third quarter and watches what Mendoza did before throwing a 19-yard touchdown pass to tight end Ian Thomas, he might recognize the move the young quarterback busted out.

After all, Mendoza borrowed it from Rodgers.

The Raiders call it the guitar fake. Watch Mendoza's hands as running back Ashton Jeanty approached Mendoza as if he was going to be handed the ball. The empty hand carries out the handoff while the other keeps the football tucked against his body.

Pittsburgh Steelers quarterback Aaron Rodgers gestures at a news conference after an NFL football game in Philadelphia, Sunday, Nov. 22, 2026. AP Photo/Chris Szagola

For a beat, the quarterback looks like a guy playing air guitar. By the time Mendoza brings the ball back into view, Thomas is wide open. The extra point tied a game the Raiders once trailed 17-0.

It makes sense that any young quarterback would pick out elements of a future Hall of Famer like Rodgers and make them their own. Even more so in the case of Mendoza, who played his first two seasons at Cal, Rodgers' alma mater.

Aaron Rodgers' NFL options dwindling after ugly Steelers divorce

Mike McCarthy explains 'ultimatum' behind decision to bench Aaron Rodgers

The troubling response to Aaron Rodgers' bombshell claims against Mike McCarthy

Pat McAfee floored by Aaron Rodgers remarks about 'unhealthy' Mike McCarthy that got him cut

"Yeah, Aaron Rodgers, he also went to Cal, so it's a little funny, but it was something that we've worked on a lot," Mendoza said.

Mendoza spent his last college season at Indiana working primarily from the shotgun, several yards behind the center, with the defense in front of him and the back alongside him.

Get under center in Klint Kubiak's offense, and the view changes in a hurry. Mendoza has to take the snap, turn his back on the defense, meet a runner coming downhill, then get his head around and find his receiver. His feet have to sell the handoff, too.

"So, if you don't have a lot of experience detailing under center handoffs, it's sometimes hard to then develop the fakes," Mendoza said.

In April, by Mendoza's own assessment, his guitar fake "looked horrendous."

Las Vegas Raiders quarterback Fernando Mendoza (15) receives instructions during the fourth quarter against the Denver Broncos in Denver, Colorado, Sunday, Nov. 22, 2026. Kate Benic/UPI/Shutterstock

"So then actually being a productive part of the offense this past Sunday was a cool moment to see the development," Mendoza said.

Imagine showing him those two clips back then. In one, Denver bites hard enough on his handoff for Thomas to get loose. In the other, the guy he borrowed it from interrupts a beef with his head coach to talk a little ball about the kid from Cal.

#2765. Global stocks rally as investors shrug off U.S. tariffs and AI jitters to 'fish for bargains'

Global stocks rallied on Thursday, staging a tentative recovery from an intense AI-driven sell-off and looked past a fresh set of tariffs unveiled by the White House on Wednesday.

U.S. markets will be closed Thursday for the Thanksgiving holiday and will have a shortened trading session on Friday. During Wednesday's regular session, the Dow gained 0.33%, the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite fell 0.62% and the S&P 500 shed 0.25%, with the major averages paring losses seen earlier in the trading day.

In Europe on Thursday, equities rose sharply. The pan-European Stoxx 600 was 1.6% higher by 12:20 p.m. in London (7:20 a.m. ET), with all sectors and major bourses in positive territory.

Germany's DAX led gains on a rise of 1.9%, while the Stoxx 600 Technology index jumped 2.5%. The regional tech index has lost around 12% so far this month, amid jitters around a potential AI bubble bursting and the ousting — and return — of Jensen Huang as Nvidia CEO.

German semiconductor firm Infineon was more than 4% higher in afternoon trading, as chip stocks notched a boost. ASMI and ASML each gained just over 3%, while Be Semiconductor added 3.5%.

Asia-Pacific markets also rose on Thursday, with regional tech stocks also rebounded sharply, led by Japanese chip-related companies.

South Korea's Kospi gained 0.29% to close at 4,711.21, while the small-cap Kosdaq index advanced 0.78% to 655.71 after the country's central bank hiked benchmark lending rates to a 2-year high in its final meeting of the year.

Japan's Nikkei 225 rose 0.86% to 57,499.16 while the Topix edged 0.11% higher to 3,750.4. Australia's S&P/ASX 200 gained 0.82% to 8,763.8.

Hong Kong's Hang Seng index was up 1.52% in its last hour of trade, while mainland China's CSI 300 edged higher marginally, closing at 3,900.55.

SoftBank Group surged more than 7%, while chip-testing equipment maker Advantest rose 4.57%. Lasertec and Disco gained , while Tokyo Electron climbed 0.59%

In South Korea, memory chip giants SK Hynix and Samsung Electronics advanced more than 2% and 0.35%, respectively. The gains followed a bruising session Wednesday amid renewed concerns over the sustainability of artificial intelligence spending.

Russ Mould, investment director at AJ Bell, labeled it a "solid day" for regional markets, suggesting investors were hunting for opportunities in the wake of an intense global sell-off.

"The rollercoaster stock market is back on an upwards path as a new round of Trump tariffs aren't as bad as feared and the US takes the day off to celebrate Thanksgiving," he said in a note on Thursday morning. "It was notable that investors cast their net wide for opportunities, fishing for bargains in the rubble following the tech sell-off and looking at other sectors in the quest for diversification."

But Mould warned investors not to be "too complacent."

"Oil prices heading higher is not good for efforts to tame inflation, neither is the prospect of retaliation by countries on the receiving end of new US tariffs," he said.

The Trump administration on Wednesday unveiled a fresh barrage of tariffs on more than a dozen trading partners, including China, the European Union and India.

Daniela Hathorn, senior financial market analyst at Capital.com, said in a Thursday note that the mood in markets was "cautious rather than uniformly negative."

"European markets are attempting to rebound after yesterday's decline, while U.S. cash markets are closed for Thanksgiving, limiting the chance to test whether Wall Street's late recovery can hold," she said. "The main focus now will be trying to determine how many further increases the Fed considers necessary in early 2027 and whether AI shares can withstand that rate path while investors continue to question the returns on heavy spending."

U.S. inflation data on Wednesday reinforced expectations of further monetary tightening by the Federal Reserve. The personal consumption expenditures price index rose 3.8% year on year in October, accelerating from 3.7% in September, while core inflation came in slightly above expectations at 3.3%.

Mark Haefele, chief investment officer at UBS Global Wealth Management, said in a note on Thursday morning that his team believes investors should maintain strategic allocations to equities.

But he advised market participants to "review current allocations and use market moves as an opportunity to rebalance, refocus, and strengthen portfolio resilience."

#2764. Barcelona set sights on Mario Hermoso

Mario Hermoso's impressive performance against PSG didn't go unnoticed in Spain.

The Roma defender, who scored the opening goal and was named UEFA's Man of the Match, is reportedly back in Barcelona's sights.

According to Fichajes.net, the Catalan club is closely monitoring the Spaniard's situation ahead of next season.

The contractual situation, however, is set to change. Roma wants to continue with Hermoso, who has become a key figure in Gasperini's defense, and significant updates on the extension negotiations have emerged in recent days.

#2763. SpaceX, Musk pledge to donate a share each to active Trump Accounts

SpaceX said Wednesday it will donate one share of the company's class A stock to every active Trump Account, and CEO Elon Musk would personally match the gift.

"SpaceX is building the infrastructure of the future and working to make life multiplanetary," the company said in a statement. "We believe ownership of the company creating these systems should extend to the next generation of Americans for their long-term benefit."

The White House announced in October that nearly 70 million Trump Accounts had been created, including more than 60 million through auto-enrollment. It's unclear how many are considered active.

Musk said in a social media post on X on Thursday that the share from SpaceX would be transferred next month, without providing further details. "The share directly from me will be transferred when my lockup expires in June next year," he added.

Eligible children must have their account claimed to enable contributions from family members, friends and employers, and to receive the government's one-time $1,000 seed contribution. Auto-enrolled children can receive philanthropic contributions without a parent or guardian claiming an account, according to TrumpAccounts.gov.

Representatives for SpaceX and the U.S. Department of the Treasury did not immediately respond to a request for further details about the planned gift.

SpaceX Chief Operating Officer Gwynne Shotwell had previously agreed to make a personal gift of over 2 million SpaceX shares to Trump Accounts, with eligible account holders expected to receive them in December.

In October, Elon Musk posted on X that he would give a share of SpaceX to every kid in America next year.

Trump Accounts, also known as 530A accounts, are open to any U.S. child under 18 with a Social Security number.

Parents, guardians, grandparents and others can collectively contribute up to $5,000 per child per year in after-tax dollars up until the year before the beneficiary turns 18. The annual contribution limit is indexed for inflation after 2027.

Since the July 4 launch, families and friends have collectively contributed more than $1 billion to Trump Accounts. Total deposits now sit at more than $9 billion, CNBC first reported.

Children born from 2025 through 2028 may receive a one-time $1,000 deposit from the Treasury. Other funds may also be available, depending on certain criteria.

The Treasury rolled out temporary regulations at the end of September that allow Trump Accounts to hold stock donations, a move officials have said is meant to encourage more philanthropic gifts.

Contributing stock can benefit donors, who are able to avoid capital gains taxes triggered by selling appreciated stock. But families can face restrictions related to selling that donated stock. The policy also means some children's wealth accumulation could be tied to the performance of individual companies.

Under U.S. Department of the Treasury guidelines, families cannot refuse stock gifted to Trump Accounts and generally cannot sell it until the earlier of five years or the end of the calendar year in which the beneficiary turns 17.

SpaceX shares have been under some pricing pressure as the company negotiates a possible merger with Elon Musk's automaker Tesla. The companies have established two special committees to handle merger negotiations, and hired advisers for a potential mega-deal.

CNBC's Sam Meredith, Kevin Breuninger, Kate Dore and Jess Dickler contributed to this story

#2762. The biggest money-making energy trade looks over. What to buy instead

Is one of the greatest money-making trades of the last few years coming to an end?

No, I'm not talking about Nvidia or any of the data center- or AI-related power companies (although they have been crushed lately too… more on that on CNBC TV. Tune in!).

I'm talking about whether the epic boom in refinery-related stocks is unwinding. Over the last year, the jump in some of these companies has been nothing short of remarkable.

That jump is largely due to massive increases in margins, earnings, and cash flow. Those have occurred because of a big jump in what is called a crack spread. That is the difference between the price of oil and the finished price of refined products such as diesel and jet fuel. For most of this year, spreads have been soaring as disruptions and attacks on facilities and shipping in the Arabian Gulf, Hormuz, and deep inside Russia have played havoc with the market. In the last week or so, however, something big has changed: many of those spreads have started to tighten.

This seismic drop in spreads is causing a selloff in the aforementioned refinery companies.

If spreads continue to fall, these stocks may well keep selling off from recent highs. The upshot for investors may be that, as the shares come down, analysts see more upside. The median price target for Phillips 66 (PSX) now indicates a 20% gain from here, while smaller-cap Par Pacific (PARR) is double that.

Here are a few other key points to focus on heading into the Thanksgiving-shortened week.

I like to keep politics as much at arm's length as possible — we all know that energy and politics (sadly) often go hand in hand. Energy policies coming from Washington, D.C., can drive — or destroy — investment dollars. With all the big shifts in the White House over the last 10 years (four years of Trump, four years of Biden, and now 2 years into Trump #2), energy policies can blow through Washington faster than a summer squall hits a wind turbine. But because federal policies can mean so much for specific companies, sectors, and investments, it's important to follow the news flow.

That said, I'd like to take a moment to introduce you to the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, known as BAAJA (and pronounced like the state in Mexico). Like much legislation, BAAJA is an obscure but important bill working its way through the American Congress. BAAJA does a lot of things, but what Power Insider readers care about is its focus on energy permitting reform. In a nutshell, BAAJA would help speed up the building of new electricity infrastructure. Again, that's an overly simplistic view of a big bill, but it needs to be on your radar. The utility trade group Edison Electric Institute came out in favor of the legislation this month after the industry received some 11th-hour changes. See more about it in our recent interview with EEI President Drew Maloney here.

The bill passed the U.S. Senate in a vote late Thursday night. Now it moves to the House. Support for its final passage is also coming from the big LNG trade group, whose executive director said he "applauds the Senate for its bipartisan effort to pass meaningful, bipartisan, and durable permitting reforms aimed at delivering greater predictability, consistency, and certainty in the permitting, production, and export process for U.S. LNG. This legislation moves us closer to untangling red tape and eliminating overlapping agency reviews that can keep U.S. energy projects delayed for years."

The Natural Gas Supply Association also chimed in, saying it urges Congress to see the bill as "a resource-agnostic and balanced reform agenda for boosting American energy security. It keeps strong environmental reviews in place, but it draws a firm line against the endless procedural delays that tie critical energy projects in knots for a decade."

In my view, passing BAAJA is an important test of Congress's appetite for helping the energy and electrical power industry, lowering costs, and speeding time to market. The old adage was correct: time is money. Literally. If BAAJA moves on through the House, we could see an upside move in some utility stocks and any company related to energy infrastructure.

Speaking of politics, the news is getting lost in the massive Anthropic (ANT) IPO, but the Texas Commission on Environmental Quality (TCEQ) — whose importance I wrote about in the last edition — just decided to resume permitting for data centers. The Texas Tribune did a nice write-up on it, and you can read it here.

It's tough to say, but it's true. The two are inextricably linked by policies created at both the federal and state levels. As I wrote in the last edition, some federally elected officials may have less impact on state-level issues — like the data center buildout — but they certainly have power over national laws. While many of you are enthralled, disgusted, or confused by sordid tales of Nvidia and Jensen-gate, keep one eye open on what happens in Washington, D.C.

Have a great week, and have a wonderful Thanksgiving, everyone.

Perhaps the big investor story — which is, like most things lately, related to Jensen-gate and Nvidia—is the sizable selloff in many AI, neocloud, and data center power-related stocks.

Painful for current shareholders, but big drops can also bring big opportunities. Wall Street believes that's the case, at least right now. Look at some of the potential upside based on the stocks' prices versus their price targets.

We spoke with the CEO of Applied Digital (APLD) about all of this on CNBC last week, and you can watch it here. He highlighted how the market may be getting these stock stories wrong because, at least with his company, he has long-term contracts with solid-credit companies. In that way, Applied Digital is more like a landlord than a "pure-play" AI company.

My take → While I'm not going to recommend a specific stock, I would add that it is critical for investors to understand the differences between these companies. While they may have some things in common, they are also different, with different business models, customers, leases, and sources of revenue.

Speaking of recommendations… here are two ideas for you:

A recent decline presents a nice opportunity in SOLV Energy (MWH). UBS analyst Jon Windham upgrades SOLV to buy from neutral, believing the market is "underappreciating several key tailwinds for SOLV given uncertainty around data center demand and higher interest rates." UBS highlights how the stock has fallen lately despite making two deals and beating and raising recent guidance. UBS says now is an "attractive entry point" for SOLV. (Side note: MWH is a great stock ticker.)

While markets overall fall, BMO remains bullish on Bloom Energy (BE). After speaking with the company, the analyst is upgrading Bloom to outperform, saying he remains "increasingly confident of BE's commercial traction" and that he still has conviction even with the recent drop in shares. While analyst Ameet Thakkar did cut his target to $230 from $260, that's still 50% upside from the current price. Bloom shares have tumbled 45% since October 1.

I was recently asked for my opinion on the price of oil. Here's my take. The market clearly wants to push oil contract prices lower and has for a few months. But, as I've said and reported for a while, don't confuse the futures (paper) price with the actual price. Yes, shipping rates and insurance rates have come down from recent highs — and there is a good amount of oil now flowing through Hormuz — but the cost of a physical barrel of crude is still well above the futures contract. If we get real, longer-lasting peace around Iran, oil prices will fall. There is plenty of oil in the world. That said, if bombs and missiles start firing again, oil prices will firm. I know that's a non-answer answer, but the oil market is truly headline-driven right now. My late good friend Boone Pickens (later in life, he didn't like being called by the "T" in his first name) used to avoid giving a set price target and instead give a directional quote, so I'll do the same: the odds of oil being lower in two months than it is today are 75%.

Last week, I had a one-on-one with Vimal Kapur, chair and CEO of Honeywell International (HON), at an RBC conference in New York City. While the conversation wasn't filmed, I can tell you that Kapur offered investors reassurance that his order book is doing very, very well. This is important because Honeywell is huge in the things that make other things work. Its sensors go into a ton of energy facilities, from refineries to LNG facilities. Honeywell is a leading indicator of spending and buildouts, so if it keeps getting orders, it's a good sign for macro energy.

Bottom line: Watch Honeywell stock as a potential "tell."

This week's Inside Line interview takes a different approach. We hit our friend Craig Johnson up for actionable insights into energy and stocks. Craig is managing director and chief market technician at Piper Sandler. He recently appeared on Power Lunch to talk about markets and stocks. He mentioned being bullish on energy, so we wanted to dig in just a little bit more.

This week, we had the pleasure of chatting with Balaji Krishnamurthy, president of Chevron Australia. We spoke at length about natural gas, the impact of disruptions, and long-term demand from Chevron customers in the Asia-Pacific region.

This is a bit of a local RBI for the New York City area, but one I felt was important to highlight. New York's electric grid operator just came out with a massive warning for everyone in our region: in just a couple of years, there may not be enough electricity supply to power our needs. A new report shows a deficit in seven years, with a nearly 2 GW power hole in a decade. That's a number of big power plants' worth of energy. Get your solar panels on and buy that backup generator! I'll call this one random, but scary.

Catch up with more on energy including interviews and video content from CNBC and Power Insider.

#2761. Knicks’ Pacome Dadiet has big night after Mike Brown calls him out

Mike Brown sent a warning to Pacôme Dadiet:

Dadiet responded with his best game of the season Wednesday, dropping seven points in 14 minutes of the Knicks' 121-102 win over the Cavaliers — including a couple of big buckets in the fourth quarter.

Pacome Dadiet, who scored seven points off the bench, goes up for a layup during the Knicks' 121-102 win over the Cavaliers on Nov. 25, 2026, at Madison Square Garden. Vincent Carchietta-Imagn Images

"I didn't think he played hard in the game before," Brown said. "And I actually called him into my office and showed him some film. I told him, 'As a young guy, if you don't play hard, I can't play you.'

"And I said, 'Believe me, I got a lot of guys I can go to. I'm not afraid to go to somebody else. So the least you could do is play hard.' And I showed him how he wasn't. And I thought [Wednesday], he played hard."

OG Anunoby, who is recovering from a calf strain, progressed to individual on-court work Wednesday morning, according to Brown.

Anunoby missed his third straight game with the injury, sitting out Wednesday's game.

#2760. Spain’s Pablo Hernández de Cos set to lead European Central Bank

Pablo Hernández de Cos is set to succeed Christine Lagarde as president of the European Central Bank next year.

He received backing from euro zone leaders at the European Council Summit, multiple media reported on Thursday.

The role still requires formal appointment after consultation with the European Parliament and the ECB's Governing Council, but neither has veto power and the decision follows intense political wrangling to reach a consensus on who should serve the eight-year term.

Reuters reported that de Cos was set for the nomination after the Netherlands agreed to back Klaas Knot, the only other candidate, citing European diplomatic sources.

De Cos is a 55-year-old economist and career central banker who rose through the ranks of the Bank of Spain, starting as a researcher in the 1990s and working in various departments before becoming governor from 2018 to June 2024.

He chaired the Basel Committee on Banking Supervision from 2019 to 2024, and has been General Manager of the Basel-based Bank for International Settlements since July 2025.

Both de Cos and his main rival for the ECB top job, Knot, were widely seen as candidates with deep knowledge and experience in monetary policy and financial stability — attributes that took on particular importance at a time of resurgent inflation, fragmentation and political uncertainty.

Lagarde had extensive experience in financial institutions but was criticized by some early in her tenure for a lack of central banking experience.

De Cos gained sufficient political backing in closed-door discussions and negotiations since Lagarde said she would step down on Feb. 7, 2027, over seven years after she became the first woman to helm the ECB. That was despite a final push by the Netherlands at the European Council meeting this week backing Knot.

Representatives from the euro area's biggest economies — Germany, France, Italy, Spain and the Netherlands — have been horse-trading as they look to secure representation on the central bank's six-member Executive Board.

Influential roles as chief economist, expected to go to Germany's Markus Brunnermeier, and head of the Markets Portfolio, for which France's Laurence Boone has reportedly been tapped, also change hands in 2027.

Analysts say de Cos's commentary and actions over more than two decades at Spain's central bank paint him as moderately dovish — inclined to favor lower interest rates to support economic growth — but also as a pragmatist who has been willing to adapt.

That has included support for strong rate hikes during the energy price shock that followed Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022, but also giving staunch backing for rates below zero and the huge stimulus package launched by then-ECB President Mario Draghi to reboot the euro zone economy in 2019.

De Cos quipped in 2024 that he was an "owl" after being asked if he was a hawk or a dove, emphasizing his focus on data and the situation at hand.

"It is true that Hernández de Cos supported the ECB's uber-dovish monetary policy during the period when inflation was persistently below target. However, his monetary policy views may well be regime-dependent," Christian Kopf, head of fixed income and foreign exchange at Union Investment, said in emailed comments.

"We are now clearly in a higher-inflation regime, with core inflation in the euro area expected to remain above the ECB's target through 2029."

Kopf added that de Cos was the candidate with the "strongest technical understanding of monetary policy and central banking," which he said was important at a time when the world is "witnessing an assault on central bank independence, not only in the United States but also in countries such as Japan."

"By appointing a career technocrat such as Hernández de Cos as ECB President, the European Council would send a strong signal that Europe will not waver in its commitment to price stability and that the euro will remain a hard currency," Kopf said.

Spain's de Cos as president, Germany's Brunnermeier as chief economist and France's Boone as head of market operations represent a "carefully crafted package deal," Kopf also noted, because it balances representation from northern and southern European countries. The result was partially due to the knock-on effects of the German government pushing hard for the chief economist position, he said.

Along with a desire among politicians for influence, a north-south balance is seen as important as the northern countries are seen as leaning more hawkish — more inclined to opt for rate hikes and restrictive policy to tame inflation — while southern are perceived as more dovish.

John Orchard, chairman of the Digital Monetary Institute at think tank OMFIF, said that as a "central banker's central banker," de Cos had "managed to earn respect from typically hawkish northern observers."

Another supportive factor of de Cos's candidacy was the stellar growth rate of many of those southern economies in recent years.

Once the "sick men" of the euro area's 2010s sovereign debt crisis, Portugal, Italy, Spain and Greece have become some of the bloc's strongest growth drivers in recent years.

Factors such as strong tourism, relative political stability, increased foreign investment and strengthening public finances have boosted southern economies. Meanwhile, the euro zone's traditional powerhouse Germany has stuttered amid soaring industrial costs and a struggling autos market.

Orchard said that some believed that Lagarde's presidency had ushered in an "esprit de corps" at the ECB, with policymakers focused on the interests of the whole European economy.

That would, in theory, reduce the importance of nationality among leadership and of traditional notions of hawkish northern nations and dovish southern ones. However, Orchard also noted the recurrent theme of national representation in the recent debate and the perception of Knot as a hawkish choice.

Diverging performance is a core challenge for any leader of the ECB, which must set monetary policy for 21 economies all growing at different speeds and with their own domestic priorities and inflationary pressures.

But it has become particularly pressing this year as the bloc's second-biggest economy, France, is teetering on the edge of its own potential debt crisis.

Moves in French markets and whether the ECB needs to take more extreme measures beyond continuing to urge fiscal discipline will remain in focus for de Cos.

The ECB Executive Board's view of what measures could be taken to prevent market disorder and under what conditions was a key topic under discussion when choosing who should fill the roles, according to insiders.

Despite political efforts to pass a budget that will reduce its budget deficit to around 5% of GDP, investors have been driving up French borrowing costs on concerns over its mounting public debt-to-GDP ratio, high budget deficit and contracting economy.

Risks are being compounded by political uncertainty ahead of France's presidential election in April, which is stymying business activity and raising wider questions about EU unity as far-right and far-left candidates pull ahead.

Rising French yields and the widening spread in France's borrowing costs over Germany's have led to speculation over whether the ECB might need to deploy tools such as liquidity injections or its never-used Transmission Protection Instrument to protect other countries from contagion and support the euro.

This would be politically controversial unless France agreed to stringent fiscal discipline measures akin to those that southern nations signed up to during the Sovereign Debt Crisis — which could be complicated if far-right presidential frontrunner Marine Le Pen, who has a combative relationship with EU institutions, wins.

Ken Egan, head of European sovereigns at ratings agency KBRA, said fragmentation was always a challenge for the ECB, which does not want to see divergent financing conditions and high sovereign spreads across the bloc.

However, he said that in the case of France he believed we were "some way from having a serious conversation about ECB intervention."

"If anything, sovereign spreads have pulled back a bit and tightened, and some measures of market liquidity have also improved in recent weeks," he said by email.

"That doesn't mean the underlying risks have disappeared, but I think it's important to distinguish between periods of market volatility and a more fundamental breakdown in monetary policy transmission."

De Cos also takes up the mantle as Europe faces yet another surge in price pressures.

Euro area inflation rose to 4% in October, its highest level in more than three years. Energy was the biggest contributor, as the Iran war drives up oil and gas prices and tightens the supply of refined fuels.

That leaves the central bank facing a difficult policy balance, preventing higher energy costs from spreading into wages and wider prices while avoiding unnecessary damage to growth.

De Cos will inherit major supranational projects, including plans for a digital euro and Europe's long-delayed capital-markets integration. In a recent speech, Lagarde warned that each year without capital markets integration meant "more of our savings are invested abroad and more of our growing firms look elsewhere for funding."

France is not the only euro area country heading for a pivotal election next year, with votes in Finland, Greece, Italy, Estonia, Slovakia and Slovenia also expected, adding to the politically uncertain climate.

It is also not the only country grappling with higher government borrowing costs, public debt and a struggle to find fiscal headroom, a growing problem among many developed countries.

The ECB's Financial Stability Review meanwhile warned this month of mounting risks from European investors' growing exposure to the U.S. economy, and equity and credit markets' vulnerability to AI.

Outgoing head Lagarde, meanwhile, told the European Banking Congress in Frankfurt this month that her biggest concern was now cyber threats, an area she said policymakers had not paid enough attention.

"The one [worry] that keeps me awake, and many of you awake ​as well ... ​is ⁠AI applied together with cyber threat," she said.

#2759. Brad Keselowski Drops Cryptic Message on NASCAR Future Amid Racing Community’s Impatience

A post celebrating five completed rocket engines was an unlikely place for NASCAR fans to go looking for Silly Season news. Yet when Brad Keselowski replied to Stoke Space's November 19 update with a single salute emoji, it was enough to draw another demand from the racing community. Jared Gray responded the following night: "Give us some news brad!)"

The exchange offered no clue about Keselowski's next destination. But the impatience behind Gray's reply has only grown as the 2027 season approaches, and Keselowski has now offered a more revealing response.

On November 26, he returned to the conversation with a post on X, using a line from Rush's "Tom Sawyer": "He knows changes aren't permanent, but change is…", before adding: "Working hard, making progress. Will let everyone know what's up when the time is right." The message suggests movement, but it is not an announcement of a deal or a new team.

The uncertainty stems from Keselowski's departure from RFK Racing. The 2012 Cup Series champion confirmed in September that he would leave the No. 6 Ford at the end of 2026, ending his five-year run as a driver and co-owner. He made clear then that he was nowhere near ready to retire and still had plenty he wanted to accomplish in NASCAR.

Brad's silence is speaking volumes. Stay tuned to our NASCAR newsletter for the latest on what's next for one of the sport's most scrutinized figures.

The split became more formal on October 31, when RFK and Keselowski verbally agreed to separate their business arrangements. He stepped away from the competition department and governing board, but remained a passive investor while working to sell his 20% stake. NASCAR.com reported the agreement and his next steps.

His news conference that day also clarified his choices. Keselowski said he had several Cup opportunities for 2027 and beyond and wanted to remain involved as a driver and manager. His options, however, were not limited to another seat. He discussed ownership opportunities with other teams and a possible move to the television booth.

He also left the door open to a full-time return to the Craftsman Truck Series, saying nothing was off the table. When asked whether six was the number of options he was considering, he confirmed it.

One complication still hanging over his RFK exit involves Fenway Sports Group's interest in Cadillac's Formula 1 operation. FSG, an RFK co-owner, reportedly wanted to buy a stake in the Cadillac project, but a contractual restriction tied to its NASCAR partnership required Keselowski's permission before it could pursue a motorsports investment outside NASCAR.

Although he has agreed to unwind his RFK ownership, the sale of his remaining stake has not been completed. EssentiallySports reported that Keselowski had allowed discussions but had not approved a binding deal. FSG has since lost its exclusive negotiating window, although it remains among the interested buyers. The Cadillac pursuit is separate from Keselowski's racing plans, but it shows why the final pieces of his RFK separation still matter.

A return to Dodge has also been floated because Keselowski won his 2012 championship with the manufacturer. But he told reporters in October that he had not had a discussion with Dodge or Ram, while remaining open to all four manufacturers.

Kaulig Racing, named as Dodge's flagship Cup team for 2027, has since announced AJ Allmendinger and Ty Dillon for its two full-time entries. CEO Chris Rice said the organization had not discussed signing Keselowski beyond the speculation circulating on social media.

This does not mean Keselowski is short of possibilities. He has 36 Cup wins, a championship, and experience rebuilding RFK while holding an ownership stake. He has said he wants a role that allows him to compete for wins and championships, not simply remain on the grid.

With his departure from the No. 6 finalized and several routes still available, the next decision is his to make.

The Stoke Space salute may have been no more than a passing acknowledgement. The real update came later, when Keselowski said he is making progress but will speak only when the time is right.

For now, that is the closest NASCAR's impatient audience has to an answer.

#2757. Nvidia surge: What investors should do with the stock and the AI trade now

Nvidia shares gapped higher at the open by more than 30%, on pace for its biggest one-day gain in 23 years on Monday, following the news just before the bell that former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang is going to be reinstated.

Investors and traders said that while this reflex rally may have more to run, cautiousness is warranted on Nvidia and the AI trade because of the overhang of investigations into the alleged Chinese espionage involving the company and others, as well as the possibility for data center moratoriums as Democrats take control of Congress and key governor posts in January.

"Am I as bullish on Nvidia now as I was on Friday? Of course not, you can't be," said Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, which counted Nvidia as its largest position as he bought on the dip over the last few weeks. "I'm trimming back."

"All the same issues that were here before Jensen resigned are still here," he added.

The day before Huang resigned on Nov. 12 as CEO, Nvidia closed at $173.73. It's intraday high on Monday was $171.50, up from its recent low around $125 as it was selling off in the wake controversies. The VanEck Semiconductor ETF closed at $506.96 the day before his resignation. It reached a high of $469.35 before losing steam as trading wore on Monday.

Mike Khouw, YieldMax strategist at Tidal Financial Group, told CNBC on Monday that there were "sighs of relief," "applause" and even "cheering" among investors at the news of Huang's potential reinstatement. But he also said there are still considerable risks facing the AI trade.

Jensen Huang was not the only problem at Nvidia, he said, and there are still many vulnerabilities in the overall AI trade, which should give investors pause. Credit-sensitive companies are still at risk of faltering, Khouw added.

The national security implications of the scandal that has rocked Nvidia and the global AI trade more broadly aren't going away any time soon even as the Trump administration assured the Nvidia board there were none. Investors on Wall Street are expressing strategic concerns about ways to play the tech space just as lawmakers in Washington are demanding answers.

Democrats are demanding answers on what exactly happened with Nvidia and the Chinese government.

"The reckless decision to bring Jensen Huang back as CEO does not change the fact that Mr. Huang, NVIDIA executives, and the company's board still owe Congress answers about the company's compliance with U.S. export controls," Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-Mass.) said in a Monday statement. "The Trump Administration also needs to explain to Congress what 'input' was given and by which senior officials in the United States government to support his reinstatement."

House Ways and Means ranking member Richard Neal (D-Mass.) stressed the importance of Congressional oversight in determining the best course of action to move forward. The outcome of any Congressional investigations will likely be crucial for Nvidia's long-term viability as an investment opportunity, especially since the company does so much international business.

"This is an export control issue," Neal told CNBC on Monday. "Congress is not subservient to this president or any other president as it relates to trade and tariff policies. This is a trade related issue."

Many of the same Democrats will call for a slowdown in the data center buildout, a strong issue for the public partly responsible for their Blue Wave earlier this month.

"You're going to have to work your way through what these data center moratoriums all mean," said Niles. "Now I think the broader market keeps going but I am not as convinced on the move in the data-center centric names from here."

To be sure, many Wall Street analysts were rushing to upgrade Nvidia's stock on Monday after Huang's reinstatement. Most restored price targets well above $200 with one as high as $270. But many traders think the rebound may fail before getting back to those levels.

Famed short seller Michael Burry, who made a boatload betting against Nvidia with put options as it cratered, told his Substack audience that he thinks the rebound here could be "outsized," especially with low volume in the market overall because of the Thanksgiving holiday this week.

But he too sees the rally eventually losing steam.

"Jensen being back without much more information might even restore NVDA to the high 100s," Burry wrote. "I won't short it on this information I have today until it gets closer to or maybe hurdles $200."

#2756. Even the price of toys is on the rise. How Americans are scaling back holiday plans

As Amanda Brecht planned how her family would spend Thanksgiving this year, she had to make some difficult decisions.

Because of high gas prices, the 30-year-old decided she wouldn't drive across her home state of Michigan to celebrate with extended family. Instead, Brecht and her husband will spend the holiday with just their three kids. And with food prices rising, they'll be skipping appetizers and serving turkey breast instead of a whole bird.

"Thanksgiving is definitely going to look different this year," Brecht said. "Money doesn't grow on trees. You can only do so much."

With inflation accelerating, Americans like Brecht will see higher prices almost everywhere they look this holiday season. At a time of year synonymous with food, gifts and big spending, many consumers are instead pinching pennies, paring back their plans and making tough choices about how they'll celebrate.

Some are raiding their savings accounts and running up debt on credit cards. They're reporting downbeat outlooks about the economy in surveys. And they've become more selective with spending as inflationary pressures mount.

"Families are facing some tough choices this year," said Alexander Smith, an associate professor who researches behavioral economics at the Worcester Polytechnic Institute. "They might have to cut back on holiday spending in whatever way that they can."

A classic Thanksgiving feast will be harder to pull off as grocery prices rise.

The price of a 10-person Thanksgiving dinner increased 6% to $58.63 in 2026, according to an annual survey from the American Farm Bureau Federation. Faith Parum, the organization's deputy chief economist, said the price increase was a result of higher costs for labor and transportation.

Grocers including Walmart and Aldi have gained traction on social media by offering Thanksgiving meal bundles as a value-focused workaround to higher prices. Walmart's offering promises to feed 10 for $35, and includes a turkey and ingredients to make mashed potatoes, green bean casserole, cranberries, marshmallow-topped sweet potatoes and a pumpkin pie.

Still, some consumers will feel disappointed by having less choice in their menus due to higher prices, according to Joe Bruseulas, chief economist at tax firm RSM.

"We're missing the point here if all we're doing is focusing on the fact that Walmart will sell you a homogeneous dinner meal" at a discount, Bruseulas said. "People feel horrible that they have to go to that because the price of everything has just risen so significantly over the past five years."

Inflation is affecting more than Thanksgiving staples like turkey or stuffing. Food prices on average have jumped around 24% over the last five years, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. Some items have seen even sharper increases: Coffee and ground beef, for example, have each climbed by more than 40% in the same period despite pulling back from recent highs.

At the same time, millions of Americans lost SNAP benefits under policy changes included in President Donald Trump's "big beautiful bill." Food banks across the U.S. have seen foot traffic skyrocket this year as the reduction in federal nutrition aid coincides with rising prices in grocery aisles.

The Salvation Army said some of its food pantries in October provided double or triple the amount of groceries than they did in the same month a year ago. Demand is now so high that some food banks in Feeding America's national network are worried about running out of supplies.

"We want to make sure that our food banks are as full as they possibly can be going into the holiday season," said Linda Nageotte, Feeding America's president and operations chief. "Far too many of them have empty shelves."

High energy prices as a result of the war with Iran are also affecting Americans' holiday plans.

Slightly fewer Americans will travel at least 50 miles around the Thanksgiving holiday in 2026, marking the first year-over-year decline since the country was in lockdown from the pandemic in 2020, according to AAA. Stacey Barber, a vice president at the auto association, attributed this year's pullback in part to higher fuel costs.

AAA said that about nine out of every 10 Thanksgiving travelers will do so by car. While gas prices have come down from a May peak, they are still up about 18% year over year and could set a new record for the month of November, according to the organization's data.

Travelers are paying about 31% and 23% more for roundtrip domestic airfare around Thanksgiving and Christmas, respectively, than they did a year ago, according to booking platform Hopper. Airlines have blamed the ticket price hikes on rising jet fuel costs in the wake of the war.

"There will be a significant shell shock when they see the price of traveling," said Hayley Berg, lead economist at Hopper.

Berg said price increases would feel more noticeable on routes previously serviced by Spirit, the budget airline that shut down in May. Travelers are booking holiday flights earlier than they did in past years, a sign that prices are top of mind, she said.

Staying home for the holidays isn't a silver-bullet solution, either, as heating costs jump.

The average American household is expected to spend 7% more on home heating this winter than they did last year, according to the National Energy Assistance Directors Association. For the typical family, that equates to an increase of about $66 and a total bill for the season above $1,000.

To be sure, some families will feel it more than others. Heating oil, which is used mainly in homes in the Northeast, has jumped around 70% year over year. However, natural gas, which is one of the most common heating types in the U.S., costs about the same as last year.

Some U.S. states, like Massachusetts and New York, beefed up their home energy support programs to account for higher prices. But Mark Wolfe, NEADA's executive director, said federal aid offerings haven't been meaningfully expanded this year.

"Some of these increases are caused directly by the decision to go to war," Wolfe said. "Why isn't the federal government helping families pay these higher bills?"

The spirit of giving will take on a different meaning for consumers this year.

Retail sales during the all-important holiday season are expected to grow almost 5% this year and surpass $1 trillion for the first time, according to Bain & Company. But the consulting firm said most of that growth would come from inflation, rather than from consumers buying more products.

And Citi on Wednesday said that increased holiday spending would be mainly driven by higher earners this year. The bank said that's a result of the "K"-shaped economy, a term used to describe a scenario where better-off Americans splash out while others struggle.

Toy prices have climbed more than 9% over the past year and now sit at their highest levels since 2019, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. Manufacturers have raised prices in response to higher costs for transportation and plastic, according to Chris Byrne, an independent toy industry analyst. The artificial intelligence boom has driven up prices for the chips that power gaming devices and other electronics.

Byrne said that's causing shoppers to focus more on the quality of gifts rather than quantity.

"The toy industry always reflects the culture at large," Byrne said. "It's almost true economically as well. As inflation is rising, people are being a little bit more cautious about what they're buying."

Just 16% of respondents to a Morning Consult survey said they planned to spend more in the 2026 holiday season than they did a year ago. Respondents who were older or had lower earnings were more likely to say their budgets were shrinking, the survey found.

Some are doing away with gifts altogether. A poll from consulting firm TSG and the Electronic Transactions Association found 5% of Americans did not plan to buy any presents in 2026, up three percentage points from last year.

One shopper, Wendy Cooper, set a limit of one gift for each of her three adult children this year. For her teenage daughter, the 56-year-old Atlanta resident has scoured Five Below, the trendy value retailer, for budget-friendly presents.

"It's super stressful," Cooper said. "You have to weigh: Do you guys want lights on and a place to live? Or do you want gifts?"

Across the board, the Bureau of Labor Statistics' consumer price index reaccelerated after the U.S. first struck Iran in late February. Annual inflation rates have begun rising faster than wages in the same period, leaving consumers with less purchasing power.

Even if Americans keep spending for the holidays, economists caution that high prices during this busy shopping period can further drag on their economic outlooks. In the CNBC Voter Poll early this month, about three-fourths of participants rated the economy as poor or not good. Consumer confidence gauges had dropped significantly this year, but have regained ground in the runup to the holiday shopping season.

From her home in Northern Michigan, Brecht is doing everything she can to make Christmas feel special without breaking the bank.

The graduate student has been delivering DoorDash orders in her spare time and listing clothes on resale platforms to save up money for her kids' holiday gifts. Brecht is watching closely for discounts and will look for potential presents at thrift stores in the coming weeks.

If necessary, Brecht said she would turn to credit cards for some holiday purchases. She isn't alone: TSG and ETA's survey found that about one-third of Americans said they expected to go into debt during the holiday season, a roughly 74% increase from 2025.

"I don't really know how we're going to do it," Brecht said. "I'm just praying for good Black Friday deals."

#2755. ‘Here it comes’: Meteorologist sends brutal warning for Chiefs-Bills weather conditions

The Kansas City Chiefs and Buffalo Bills are set to face each other on Thursday night in a game that will have massive ramifications on the AFC playoff picture. Unforunately we may not get to see the star quarterbacks, Patrick Mahomes and Josh Allen, at the top of their games due to a brutal weather forecast.

According to AccuWeather, there's a 90% chance of rain this Thursday at Highmark Stadium.

"A gust at the wrong moment can push a field goal wide or send a deep pass off target. With rain, a slick football can make even a routine catch or handoff harder to secure," AccuWeather meteorologist Tyler Roys said via the Democrat & Chronicle.

Conditions for Thursday's ultra-important game will not only be wet, but things are shaping up for significant wind gusts and temperatures in the 30s.

"Here it comes…What a night for Thanksgiving Day football and the powerhouse Bills-Chiefs matchup!! In Buffalo, we expect as much as 2″ of rainfall starting late afternoon and going thru the game. Throw on top of that winds gusting over 40mph at times. Ugghh," National Weather Service meteorologist Tom Niziol said.

Unfortunately for Mahomes, the forecast comes just as the Chiefs' passing game was starting to click again after the acquisition of wide receiver Josh Downs in a trade with the Indianapolis Colts.

The superstar quarterback has been dominant in Kansas City's last two Games. He threw five touchdown passes in a 35-13 win over the Atlanta Falcons, despite being a little nervous about the state of his elbow after an injury scare, and three more in a 34-7 win over the Arizona Cardinals.

To make matters worse, star running back Kenneth Walker III has been ruled out as he continues to work his way back from a bothersome groin. It'll be interesting to see how the Chiefs and Mahomes offense respond if the weather curbs their ability to push the ball down the field.

#2754. Buyer beware? Nvidia's volatile swings expose risks of single-stock ETFs

Nvidia's latest bout of volatility is highlighting risks tied to single-stock exchange-traded funds.

Among the hardest hit: the GraniteShares 2x Short NVDA Daily ETF (NVD). The fund sank 63% on Monday after Jensen Huang got reinstated as the chip giant's CEO. Meanwhile, the firm's 2x Long NVDA ETF (NVDL) soared nearly 62% on the same day.

ETF Action founding partner Mike Akins joined "ETF Edge" as Nvidia shares soared 31% during Monday's session, wiping a large portion of the losses from the days following Huang's resignation. Shares cooled on Tuesday and Wednesday, however.

"Historically speaking, we do not see these types of large drawdowns followed by huge drives up in a healthy market," Akins said on Monday. "It usually tends to be when you're in a recession or in a bear market territory, which we're not technically in for the broader market, but we are in for this AI trade."

But it may not be time to go cold turkey on single-stock funds.

"[Leveraged ETFs are] definitely a retail trading vehicle…  I think it's OK to have a little fun trying to trade that," Akins added. "I'm certain there are people that are better at trading these than others. But on the whole, timing the market is very, very hard."

GraniteShares founder and CEO Will Rhind also joined "ETF Edge" on Monday as Nvidia single-stock short ETFs overshadowed his firm's new Anthropic launches: The 2x Short Anthropic Daily ETF (ANS) and the 2x Long Anthropic Daily ETF (AIL).

"We thought this was going to be the biggest news event of the day with Anthropic the first leveraged ETFs going live," he said. "I can tell you this is like a sideshow compared to what we're seeing with Nvidia."

Rhind suggests Nvidia's wild swings aren't dampening enthusiasm for single-stock ETFs.

"As long as there's enthusiasm in the underlying name… people will want to trade it and most likely want to trade it with leverage either on the long on the short side," added Rhind.

"Fast Money" tackled the risks tied to ETFs that are tied to single stocks, too.

"If this is somehow part of a portfolio, [then] that is a dangerous game to be playing," trader Guy Adami said on Monday.

Karen Finerman also addressed the strategy's downsides.

"Leverage is always the thing that kills. It's always leverage," said Finerman, co-founder and CEO of Metropolitan Capital Advisors, in the same interview. "You can survive anything but leverage."

As of Wednesday's close, the GraniteShares 2x Short NVDA Daily ETF plunged 55% so far this week while the GraniteShares 2x Long NVDA Daily ETF is up 31%.

#2753. There’s something to like about the way the Suns lost in Houston

The Phoenix Suns went to Space City to battle the team whose logo resembles one of those silly little patches that go on astronaut suits, ultimately losing by 12. What that final score doesn't tell you is that with 4:45 remaining, the Suns were down three points. What killed them down the stretch is what killed them throughout the game, and it shouldn't come as a surprise to any of us. Rebounding was the culprit. Or, more specifically, their inability to rebound.

Phoenix was outrebounded 51-38 overall and 20-13 on the offensive glass, but it was the 27 second-chance points they surrendered that ultimately sunk them. When Alperen Sengun pulls down 11 rebounds, and seven of them are of the offensive variety, you know you've done something wrong.

But my takeaway from this game? The impressively physical brand of basketball the Suns played. They stood up to a Rockets team that clearly had revenge on its mind as they ran up and down that bright red clowny court. After all, Phoenix had already beaten Houston earlier this season. When the Rockets came out with a chip on their shoulder, the Suns rose to the challenge and battled them throughout.

The final result comes down to two things. Houston possesses a more talented roster, and they're bigger, badder, and better on the boards. The attitude with which the Suns played, however, was something I thoroughly enjoyed watching. That's who this team was a season ago, and perhaps they needed a team like Houston to push them around a little, to jostle them enough to draw a line in the sand and say, "We're done with this."

The hope is that they carry that attitude forward. Because if they can bring that level of intensity and fight night in and night out, nobody is going to want to play that version of the Phoenix Suns. We know that because we saw it last year.

The rebounding issues aren't going away, and there will be nights when Phoenix simply doesn't possess the personnel to overcome them. But physicality and effort are things this team can control regardless of the opponent. What we witnessed in Houston was a willingness to compete on somebody else's terms without being intimidated by them. The Suns lost the basketball game, but somewhere on that obnoxiously red court, they rediscovered a little bit of their swagger.

Jalen Green did Jalen Green things against the Pelicans, logging his third Baller of the season with his 25-point performance on Monday.
